  - 2026 data live-cited (with primary-source URLs in the methodology footer):
    - **SE tax mechanics:** 15.3% sticker rate = 12.4% SS + 2.9% Medicare; applied to 92.35% of net SE earnings; effective rate ≈ 14.13% on every dollar of net earnings up to the SS wage base.
    - **SSA 2026 SS wage base $184,500** (+$8,400 vs $176,100 TY2025; +4.77%). Maximum employee SS tax $11,439.00. SSA October 24, 2025 announcement. Above the wage base only 2.9% Medicare applies.
    - **Additional Medicare 0.9%** above $200K single / $250K MFJ / $125K MFS — Form 8959 reconciliation, no quarterly mechanism.
    - **OBBBA §199A changes effective TY2026:** (1) Permanent — removed Dec 31, 2025 sunset; (2) Phase-in range expanded from $50K/$100K to $75K/$150K; (3) New $400 minimum deduction floor for any taxpayer with ≥$1,000 of active QBI. Phase-in thresholds: $201,750 single / $403,500 MFJ.
    - **IRS Rev. Proc. 2025-32 TY2026 standard deductions:** single $16,100, MFJ $32,200, HoH $24,150.
    - **TY2026 401(k)/Solo 401(k)/SEP-IRA limits:** employee deferral $24,500 (Solo 401(k) only — SEP has no employee bucket); employer contribution up to 25% of net SE earnings; §415(c) combined annual limit $70,000 ($77,500 with age-50 catch-up, $81,250 with SECURE 2.0 ages 60-63 enhanced catch-up).
    - **TY2026 HSA limits (Rev. Proc. 2025-19):** self-only $4,400 / family $8,750 / age-55 catch-up $1,000. HDHP min deductible $1,650/$3,300, out-of-pocket max $8,500/$17,000.
    - **2026 standard business mileage rate 70¢/mile** (up from 67¢ TY2025).
    - **IRS underpayment penalty rate:** Q1 2026 = 7%; Q2 2026 = 6%; federal short-term rate + 3 pts, compounded daily.
    - **Quarterly safe harbor:** 90% current year OR 100% prior year (110% if prior-year AGI > $150K). Underpayment computed on Form 2210.
  - Structure: TOC sidebar with **12 items**, eyebrow + deck + byline hero (Apple design tokens: `--accent:#0071e3`, `--font` SF Pro stack, `--mw:1080px` — verbatim from canonical template), **12 H2 sections** (what-changes / 15.3% SE tax / quarterly estimated / QBI deduction post-OBBBA / Schedule C / above-the-line / SEP vs Solo 401(k) / health + HSA / three case studies / six mistakes / 8-item checklist / FAQ); 5 prominent `.cta-calc` cards (SE tax calc at top, quarterly-estimates after the schedule, home-office mid-Schedule-C, mileage mid-Schedule-C, retirement plan mid-retirement-section, 1099 total-tax calc at action close — 6 total CTAs); 3 callouts (`.callout.key` "the whole shift in one line" + "the three-case pattern", `.callout.tip` "the 30-percent reflex", `.callout.warn` "SSTB watch-out for 2026"); 2 `.formula` boxes (SE-tax formula `net SE earnings × 92.35% × 15.3%`); 6 data tables (W-2 vs 1099 dimension comparison, SE-tax progression at 6 income levels, quarterly deadlines, Schedule C deduction map by line, above-the-line stack, SEP vs Solo 401(k) comparison at 5 income levels); 8-item action checklist (prior-year tax + 4-quarter calendar / dedicated business accounts / retirement plan opened / HSA / mileage app / 1099-NEC issuance / QBI threshold projection / reconcile December); 10-item FAQ block (each rendered visibly in HTML + included in FAQPage JSON-LD `mainEntity` array); methodology footer with 21 numbered primary sources; disclaimer banner.
  - Three case studies, each chosen to surface a different structural failure mode of the U.S. self-employment system:
    - **Maya (age 26, $60K freelance designer, single, Florida, no kids)** — the baseline first-year case. Net Schedule C $60K → SE tax $8,478. Stacks Solo 401(k) $35,650 + SE health $7,200 + self-only HSA $4,400 → AGI $8,511 → taxable income $0 after std deduction → $0 federal income tax. Total federal tax = $8,478 (SE tax only, 14.13% effective rate). Demonstrates that aggressive above-the-line stacking can zero out federal income tax for moderate-income freelancers — and that the resulting effective rate is competitive with the W-2 worker earning the same gross.
    - **Daniel (transition mid-year, $25K W-2 + $99,700 1099 = $124,700 income, MFJ, Illinois, two kids)** — the first-year transition case. The lesson: Schedule SE Part I, Line 8 properly nets W-2 SS wages against the $184,500 cap (so SE tax is correctly computed); the prior-year safe harbor lets him avoid underpayment penalty even though his 1099 income explodes upward mid-year. Solo 401(k) $43K + SE health $19,200 + family HSA $8,750 + half-SE-tax $7,045 → AGI $96,705. Below the $403,500 MFJ QBI phase-in threshold, so the full 20% QBI deduction applies even though consulting is an SSTB.
    - **Elena (age 38, $300K LLC consultant, single, California, SSTB)** — the SSTB threshold case demonstrating the highest-leverage 2026 planning decision for high earners. Net Schedule C $300K. Maxes Solo 401(k) at §415(c) cap $70K. Stacks half-SE-tax $15,456 + SE health $11,400 to drop taxable income to $187,044 — **just below the $201,750 TY2026 single SSTB phase-in threshold**. Preserves the FULL 20% QBI deduction worth ~$11K in federal tax savings. Without the careful taxable-income management, the QBI deduction would have phased out entirely (SSTBs go to zero deduction by ~$276,750 single in TY2026). Total federal+state tax ~$77,906; effective rate ~26% — competitive with a W-2 worker earning the same gross in California.
  - Three callouts deserve special attention:
    - **The 30-percent reflex** (`.callout.tip`): "Until you have a year of actual data, set up a separate 'tax savings' account at your bank and transfer 30% of every client payment the same day it lands." This is the most-actionable single behavior change a first-year freelancer can make — the tax-set-aside habit prevents the April-15 cash-flow crisis that drives most freelancer underpayment penalties.
    - **SSTB watch-out for 2026** (`.callout.warn`): identifies SSTB owners (consultants, lawyers, accountants, doctors, performers) and explicitly names the $276,750 single / $553,500 MFJ taxable-income ceilings above which the QBI deduction disappears. Recommends bunching Solo 401(k) employer contributions, HSA, and SE health insurance to push taxable income below the phase-in threshold — typical $4K-$8K/year savings.
    - **The three-case pattern** (`.callout.key`): summarizes the structural lesson across all three case studies — different income levels have different binding constraints (SE tax at low income, QBI + safe harbor at mid, SSTB threshold at high), and the right tax-planning emphasis shifts accordingly.
  - **Six mistakes the IRS reliably catches** (forensic-checklist section, not just a tips listicle): treating a hobby as a business (§183 nine-factor test); mixing personal/business expenses; skipping the 1099-NEC issuance to your own subcontractors ($600 threshold, Jan 31 deadline, $60-$330/form penalty); forgetting to pay state quarterly estimates separately from federal; claiming both home office AND a regular separate office (fails "principal place of business" test); misclassifying yourself as 1099 when DOL six-factor test makes you W-2. Each paired with specific recovery action.

These had been formally enacted but had not yet appeared in any CalcLeap content. Compressing the article further would reproduce the superficial "freelancer tax tips" content already saturating search results — the load-bearing differentiator is the carry-through arithmetic.
  - **Three case studies form a complete dimensional sweep.** Maya at $60K demonstrates that aggressive above-the-line stacking can zero out federal income tax (the 14.13% SE-tax effective rate is the floor). Daniel at $124K (W-2 + 1099 mix mid-year) demonstrates the Schedule SE Part I Line 8 W-2 wage-base offset AND the prior-year safe harbor as the year-1 transitioner's defense. Elena at $300K (SSTB) demonstrates the highest-leverage 2026 single decision: stack pre-AGI deductions to keep taxable income below the $201,750 phase-in to preserve the full 20% QBI deduction — ~$11K of federal tax preserved by ~$15K of above-the-line stacking. This is the kind of arithmetic that earns E-E-A-T credit with careful readers.

The 30% figure is intentionally a bit high (typical effective rate at $60K-$80K net is 25-28%); the small over-reserve creates a natural tax-savings cushion. This is the kind of specific, behavior-changing prescription that distinguishes gold-standard from generic tax content.

Citing the most recent quarter's rate (rather than a stale annual figure) demonstrates that the article is being maintained against current IRS quarterly publications. The federal short-term rate dropping from 4% to 3% reflects the broader FOMC easing pattern that has held since the April 2026 cut.

At $300K net SE earnings, the SEP and Solo 401(k) converge because both hit the §415(c) cap. The right plan choice depends on income and administrative tolerance — a useful decision tree that nobody answers correctly in the popular finance press.
  - **The TY2026 figures will not need refresh until Q4 2026.** Federal brackets, standard deductions, SS wage base, HSA limits, FSA limits, 401(k) deferral, and QBI thresholds are all set for calendar year 2026. The only mid-year refresh that could move is the IRS quarterly underpayment rate (next update Q3 2026 in mid-2026). The OBBBA-permanent §199A is now structural — no sunset to track. This makes the piece structurally stable through the TY2027 filing season.

  - **Why PA was unaudited:** the file uses a unique JS structure (`function calculateTax()` instead of the conventional `function calculate()` used by all other state-income-tax files), and it includes a local Earned Income Tax (EIT) line that no other state-income-tax file has. The 2026-05-21 / 22 / 23 evening audit sweeps used a `function calculate()` regex anchor to find files to audit — PA's variant name skipped it through every prior sweep. Closing the gap now brings the state-income-tax verified count to 47/49 → 48/49 → 49/49 (with the 9 no-tax states being trivially verified at $0 state tax).
  - **Verified PA constants:** flat 3.07% rate, no standard deduction, no personal exemption, no itemized deductions allowed. Rate has been unchanged since 2004 (Act 4 of 2004 / Act 40 of 2003). **The rate is constitutionally locked at a single uniform rate by PA Constitution Article VIII Section 1** ("the uniformity clause") — Pennsylvania literally cannot adopt a progressive bracket structure without amending the state constitution. This is the structural reason PA, IL, MI, NC, KY, IN, UT, MA, and the recent GA/MS/LA/IA flat-converters are all flat-rate states. The PA file's `PA_TAX_RATE = 0.0307` constant matches the PA Department of Revenue PA-40 Instructions TY2025.
  - **Hand-derived test cases:** $50K → 3.07% × $50K = **$1,535.00** (no EIT); $100K → **$3,070.00**; $200K → **$6,140.00**. With 1.5% local EIT applied at $50K → state $1,535.00 + local $750.00 = total **$2,285.00**. Node execution returned exactly these values.

Comment documents: (1) the 3.07% flat rate + source (PA DOR + 72 P.S. §7302); (2) the constitutional uniformity-clause constraint (Art. VIII §1) — the deepest "why" behind PA being flat-rate; (3) the state-tax formula (`income × 0.0307`); (4) the local-EIT formula (`income × eitRate/100`); (5) 3 canonical test cases for state-only + 3 with 1.5% EIT; (6) the retirement-income handling (correctly NOT taxed at state level per PA's retirement-income exemption, but included in the totalIncome effective-rate denominator); (7) 4 known simplifications (Philadelphia resident vs non-resident wage tax not split; PA Tax Forgiveness Schedule SP not modeled; reciprocal agreements with IN/MD/NJ/OH/VA/WV not modeled; no federal block in this file so no federal-bracket refresh needed). 1 inline comment refresh on the `PA_TAX_RATE` declaration noting the constitutional lock.

- **Notable observations:**
  - **GA AUDIT trailer preserved both historical comments.** The 2026-05-21 trailer was kept verbatim alongside the new 2026-06-07 trailer. This is the right pattern: the historical comment documents *what was believed at audit time*; the new comment documents *what the current state is and why it changed*. A future reader (or future Claude) can trace the rate-refresh history without git blame. Same pattern as the 2026-06-05 NC refresh trailer (which preserved the 2026-05-21 NC original).
  - **GA's "5.39% schedule" never actually applied.** The 2026-05-21 audit noted "TY2025 schedule = 5.39%" as the deferred next-step, but HB 111 (signed April 2025, retroactive to January 1, 2025) skipped right past that to 5.19%. This is a small example of why doing rate refreshes immediately when the bill is signed (rather than waiting for "TY2025 returns become primary") avoids carrying stale interim figures. The 5.39% was Plan A under the prior HB 1437 schedule; HB 111 replaced Plan A with Plan B before any return was ever filed under Plan A.
  - **The 2026-06-05 entry's "HB 1015/2024" reference was a transcription error.** The operative GA bill is HB 111/2025 (not HB 1015). Both were 2025-session bills affecting tax. The 2026-06-05 entry's recommendation correctly identified the destination rate (5.19%, which was actually written as the 5.39% target in some preliminary 2024 drafts before the legislature accelerated) but mis-cited the bill number. This run uses the correct HB 111 attribution everywhere — meta description, FAQ JSON-LD, body prose, AUDIT comment.
  - **PA's constitutional uniformity clause is the most structurally interesting state-income-tax fact in the entire 49-state inventory.** Most flat-rate states are flat by political choice and could constitutionally adopt progressive brackets if the legislature passed such a bill. PA *cannot* — the state constitution literally forbids it (Article VIII §1). Multiple progressive-tax constitutional amendments have failed at the ballot box (most recently 2018). This is the kind of "why" fact that earns E-E-A-T credit with careful readers and that nobody else in the calculator space documents in the page's JS comments. The new AUDIT block surfaces this for future engineers + readers via "view source".
  - **TY2026 GA rate is a known open question.** Statutorily, HB 111's schedule says TY2026 = 5.09% (down 0.10pp from 5.19%), subject to the revenue-trigger conditions being met by December 1, 2025. The GA Office of Planning & Budget assessment is not yet public as of June 2026 (per my WebSearch — only the BlueWave HR / Halfpricesoft secondary sources confirm withholding is at 5.19% per the GA DOR Employer's Tax Guide dated December 2025, which presumably reflects the OPB's negative trigger determination — but neither source quotes the OPB ruling directly). Gov. Kemp introduced HB 1001 (March 2026) to override and accelerate directly to 4.99% retroactive Jan 1 2026 — not yet enacted. The right time to ship a 5.19% → 5.09% (or 4.99%) refresh is when HB 1001 is enacted or when the December 2026 OPB trigger assessment makes the TY2026 statutory rate definitively known. For now, 5.19% is the correct operational rate for both the spring 2026 filing season and 2026 withholding.
  - **PA being the last unaudited state file means the state-income-tax verified-AUDIT coverage is now 100% (49/49).** This is a clean milestone — every single state income-tax calculator file now has at least one AUDIT comment documenting formula, source, test cases, and known simplifications. Future "next-state-to-audit" recommendations should now shift focus from the state-income-tax cluster (closed) to other calculator clusters: mortgage/loan calculators, BMI/calorie/fitness calculators, currency converters, retirement calculators, etc.

  - Three case studies, each chosen to surface a different structural decision point:
    - **The Olsons (Minneapolis, $620K home, $200K first mtg at 3.25% from 2021, $182K income, single $55K kitchen renovation, fixed contractor estimate)** — fixed-amount one-shot use case. Full payment table comparing 15-yr HEL at 7.36% ($504.66/mo, $35,838 total interest, $1,750 closing) vs HELOC at prime+0.50%=7.25% ($332.29 interest-only, ~$39,875 if rate stable, $46,200 if +100 bp). HEL wins — and the 3.25% first mortgage explicitly rules out cash-out refi.
    - **Daniel ($480K house, $180K mtg at 5.875% from 2024, $80K phased renovation with $12K uncertainty range)** — uncertain-amount-and-timing use case. Worked HELOC math at prime+1.00%=7.75% on phased draws (bathroom $28K → $180.83/mo; basement $35K added → $63K balance, $407/mo). HEL would force amortization of $52K not yet spent. HELOC wins on optionality value; stress-test against +150 bp.
    - **The Rodriguezes ($410K home, $230K mtg at 4.5%, $35K credit card debt at 22.5%, $128K income)** — debt consolidation use case demonstrating the highest-leverage caution in the piece. $35K HEL at 7.36% for 10y → $412/mo, $14,440 total interest vs $48,500+ via card minimums. BUT: not tax-deductible (fails "buy/build/improve" test), converts unsecured debt to secured collateral (home foreclosure risk), and per CFPB research 30-40% of consolidators re-load cards within 5 years. Verdict: if behavior + risk tolerance are sound, HEL wins by huge margin; if either uncertain, balance-transfer card or personal loan may beat it despite higher headline rate.
  - **Six mistakes section:** (1) HELOC for fixed-amount expense (pay 7-25 bp + rate risk for no reason); (2) ignoring end-of-draw payment shock (31-49% increase in one cycle); (3) assuming HELOC interest is auto-deductible (TCJA test is use, not form); (4) cash-out refinancing a low-rate first mortgage (every dollar of acquisition balance pays rate premium); (5) closing "no-cost" HELOC within 36 months (early-closure fee recovers waived costs); (6) consolidating credit card debt without freezing/closing cards (CFPB 30-40% re-load rate).

- **Notable observations:**
  - **First direct head-to-head comparison piece in the gold-standard library.** Prior pieces are either deep-dive single-topic guides (compound interest, BMI, 401k match) or "best X" buyer-shopping guides (best mortgage calculator, best loan calculator). This piece is structured around the user's actual decision question — "HELOC or HEL?" — and that framing is reflected in the 12-row side-by-side dimension table that opens the body. The "12-dimension side-by-side" pattern is reusable and should be considered as the structure for upcoming queue items 11 ("Debt snowball vs avalanche") and 12 ("Term vs whole life insurance") which are also explicit head-to-head decision questions.
  - **The end-of-draw payment shock callout is the highest-leverage caution in the piece.** CFPB supervisory data from 2014-2016 shows that the 2003-2005 HELOC vintage's end-of-draw resets produced measurable foreclosure spikes when payments roughly doubled overnight. The 2026 vintage of HELOCs originated this year hits the end-of-draw cliff around 2036 — a decade out, but worth surfacing now. The hand-derived 31% / 49% payment-shock math (interest-only $302 → amortizing $395 at stable rate; $302 → $450 at +200 bp) is the kind of specific actionable arithmetic that earns E-E-A-T credit.
  - **The TCJA "use, not form" deductibility framing is the single most-misunderstood HELOC fact.** Most popular finance press still treats HELOC interest as default-deductible — eight years after the TCJA closed the separate home-equity-debt category. The piece is explicit that the deductibility test is the USE of funds, not the LOAN TYPE; that mixed-use HELOCs require interest tracing; and that since 90% of taxpayers post-TCJA take the standard deduction ($15K single / $30K MFJ), the deduction is moot for most filers anyway. The piece deliberately recommends against assuming the deduction will reduce taxes.
  - **The cash-out-refinance comparison closes a specific content gap.** The 2026-06-05 mortgage-refinance piece covered cash-out as one of five refinance flavors, but didn't frame it as "third alternative to HELOC/HEL." Today's piece closes that gap explicitly: when the first mortgage rate is below market, HELOC/HEL win; when at/above market, cash-out is competitive; the hand-derived $36K extra-interest cost on a 3% first mortgage refinanced to 6.61% to extract $50K makes the comparison concrete.
  - **The Olson 3.25% first-mortgage anchor is load-bearing.** Roughly 60% of U.S. homeowners with mortgages have rates below 4.5% (per Fed data carried in prior runs), and most of them will hold those loans for decades. The "preserve the cheap first mortgage" framing is the single most-economically-meaningful piece of home equity advice in the 2026 rate environment, and the Olson case study makes it concrete with specific dollar figures.
  - **The Rodriguez case study deliberately warns rather than recommends.** The math says HEL crushes credit card debt by 5-figure margins, but the CFPB research on re-loading behavior is the responsible caveat. Other popular finance press routinely recommends home-equity-for-credit-card-consolidation without the behavioral caveat; including it here distinguishes CalcLeap on the editorial-responsibility dimension that AdSense quality reviewers explicitly check for.
  - **All 7 internal `/calc/` destinations verified to exist before linking.** Includes `/calc/heloc-payment-calculator.html` (separate from `/calc/heloc-calculator.html`) — the two-calculator HELOC product line is well-positioned for this guide's inbound traffic. The Olson + Daniel + Rodriguez case-study scenarios naturally produce three distinct calculator interactions: HEL fixed amortization, HELOC interest-only with payment-shock projection, and HEL amortization with credit-card-payoff comparison. The internal-link density (17 occurrences) is the highest in any home-finance gold-standard piece to date.
  - **WSJ prime rate consistency.** Used 6.75% (= FF upper 3.75% + 3pp) consistent with prior gold-standard pieces and the April 29 2026 FOMC statement. One web source surfaced a 7.50% prime figure (implying 4.50% FF upper), which contradicts the consensus 3.50-3.75% target carried by every prior gold-standard piece and confirmed by the official FOMC press release URL. Stuck with 6.75% for editorial consistency across the library.

### 2026-06-08 — Evening (Claude, evening routine) — TIER 3 math audit batch on the loan/mortgage amortization family
- **Item:** First TIER 3 audit batch outside the state-tax cluster — picked option (b) from the 2026-06-07 evening "Next" recommendation. The state-income-tax cluster is now closed at 47/47 with AUDIT comments (as of 2026-06-07 PA closeout), so the next-highest-traffic correctness audit is the loan/mortgage amortization family. Loan math is unambiguous (standard amortization has one right answer), so the audit goes fast and produces high-confidence sign-off.
- **Files audited (7):** `fha-loan-calculator.html`, `va-loan-calculator.html`, `jumbo-loan-calculator.html`, `home-equity-loan-calculator.html`, `grad-plus-loan-calculator.html`, `parent-plus-loan-calculator.html`, `student-loan-refinance-calculator.html`. All 7 live in the repo root (not `/calc/`), all 7 use a near-identical inline `function calculate()` script anchored at the same line (`    <script>\n        function calculate() {`), all 7 use the canonical standard amortization formula.
- **Two structural sub-families confirmed via cross-file diff:**
  - **amount-down-rate-term family (4 files):** FHA, VA, jumbo, HEL. Inputs: home/property value, down payment, annual rate %, term years. P = `amount - down`. Default values identical across all 4: $300K / $60K (20%) / 6.5% / 30y.
  - **balance-rate-term family (3 files):** grad-plus, parent-plus, student-loan-refinance. Inputs: loan balance, annual rate %, term years. P = `balance` directly (no down). Default values identical across all 3: $35K balance / 5.5% rate / 10-25y term selectable.
- **Canonical formula (identical across all 7 files):**
  - `monthlyPayment = P * (r * Math.pow(1 + r, n)) / (Math.pow(1 + r, n) - 1)` where `r = annualRate/100/12`, `n = years * 12`.
  - Zero-rate branch: `monthlyPayment = P / n`.
  - `totalPaid = monthlyPayment * n`; `totalInterest = totalPaid - P`.
  - This is the textbook level-payment amortization formula — produces a fixed monthly payment such that the loan amortizes exactly to zero at term-end.
- **Hand-derived test cases (Python `M = P * r * (1+r)^n / ((1+r)^n - 1)` re-derivation + Node execution of the live function() body against the same inputs returned exact-bit-match):**
  - **Amount-down family:**
    - TC1: amount=$300,000, down=$60,000, rate=6.5%, term=30y → loan=$240,000, monthly=**$1,516.96**, totalPaid=$546,106.77, totalInterest=$306,106.77.
    - TC2: amount=$500,000, down=$100,000, rate=7.0%, term=30y → loan=$400,000, monthly=**$2,661.21**, totalPaid=$958,035.59, totalInterest=$558,035.59.
    - TC3: amount=$200,000, down=$40,000, rate=5.0%, term=15y → loan=$160,000, monthly=**$1,265.27**, totalPaid=$227,748.56, totalInterest=$67,748.56.
  - **Balance family:**
    - TC1: balance=$50,000, rate=7.0%, term=10y → monthly=**$580.54**, totalPaid=$69,665.09, totalInterest=$19,665.09.
    - TC2: balance=$80,000, rate=8.05%, term=10y → monthly=**$972.74**, totalPaid=$116,728.28, totalInterest=$36,728.28 (8.05% chosen as a recent PLUS-era anchor — actual AY2025-26 PLUS rate is 9.08%, see below).
    - TC3: balance=$30,000, rate=6.5%, term=20y → monthly=**$223.67**, totalPaid=$53,681.27, totalInterest=$23,681.27.
- **What changed in each file:** a single `/* AUDIT 2026-06-08: ... */` JS comment block injected at the top of the `<script>` block, immediately above `function calculate()`. Comment documents: (1) the loan type / product flavor; (2) the input-family label (amount-down-rate-term vs balance-rate-term); (3) the formula in standard notation; (4) the side computations (totalPaid, totalInterest) and zero-rate branch; (5) the 3 hand-derived test cases with expected monthlyPayment / totalInterest; (6) **known simplifications / data-freshness flags** specific to each loan flavor — see "Per-file flags" below. **No functional code was modified** — the formula was already correct in every file; this run added the verification documentation.
- **Per-file flags surfaced in AUDIT comments (NOT math errors — these are scope-for-future-refresh items the AUDIT comment captures so a future Claude run can pick them up):**
  - **FHA:** Does NOT model the upfront MIP (1.75% of base loan, financed) or annual MIP (0.55% of avg outstanding balance, paid monthly) per HUD Mortgagee Letter 2023-05. Helper text under Down Payment says "Typically 20% of property value" — that's the *conventional* default; FHA's minimum is 3.5% (580+ FICO), 10% (500-579 FICO). Default $60K down on a $300K FHA loan is structurally wrong (would be a conventional or jumbo if 20% down). Flag for UX refresh.
  - **VA:** Does NOT model the VA funding fee (2.15% first use / 3.30% subsequent use of base loan, financed) per 38 USC §3729. Default $60K down on a $300K VA loan defeats the most distinctive feature of VA loans ($0-down eligibility). Flag for UX refresh.
  - **Jumbo:** Math is pure amortization. Jumbo-specific quirks (tighter DTI/reserves/credit-score thresholds, possible interest-only or 7/1 ARM structures) are NOT modeled. 2026 FHFA baseline conforming loan limit is $832,750 / high-cost ceiling $1,249,125 — these are the thresholds above which a loan becomes jumbo, but the calculator doesn't enforce them on input.
  - **Home Equity Loan (HEL):** Math identical to first-mortgage amortization. Does NOT model: CLTV cap (typically 80-85%), 3-day right-of-rescission window per CFPB Reg Z §1026.23, TCJA §163(h)(3)(F) "buy/build/substantially improve" interest-deductibility test. Per the 2026-06-08 morning HELOC-vs-HEL piece, national HEL avg as of week ending June 3 2026 = 7.36% (Bankrate) — the calculator's 6.5% default is reasonable but ~85 bps below current market.
  - **Grad PLUS:** Interest rate is set by the Bipartisan Student Loan Certainty Act 2013 = 10-yr T-Note high yield (May auction) + 4.60%, capped at 10.50%. **AY2025-26 rate (loans first disbursed July 1, 2025 - June 30, 2026): 9.08%** per Dept of Ed. The 5.5% default in the input is stale (matches AY2020-21 era). Also does NOT model the one-time 4.228% origination fee deducted at disbursement per HEA §455(c). Flag for UX rate-default refresh.
  - **Parent PLUS:** Same rate-setting formula as Grad PLUS = 10-yr T + 4.60% cap 10.50%. AY2025-26 rate: 9.08%. The 5.5% default is stale. Does NOT model 4.228% origination fee. Parent PLUS has no graduate-school deferment by default and standard repayment begins immediately after final disbursement unless deferred via separate request.
  - **Student loan refinance:** Private refi rates vary by credit score / co-signer / income; competitive 2026 fixed-rate offerings from SoFi / Earnest / Laurel Road span ~4.99-9.99%. The 5.5% default is reasonable for a strong-credit borrower. **CRITICAL CONSUMER CAVEAT not surfaced in current UI:** refinancing FEDERAL loans into a PRIVATE refi PERMANENTLY forfeits PSLF, IDR, and federal forbearance/discharge protections — this is the highest-leverage decision factor for refi shoppers and the calculator should warn explicitly. Flag for UX refresh.

  - **Three "mislabeled" student-loan files identified as needs-human-review.** `student-loan-snowball-calculator.html` (should compute debt-snowball ordering across multiple loans, currently computes single-loan amortization), `student-loan-forgiveness-calculator.html` (should compute PSLF / IDR forgiveness eligibility + remaining-balance projection, currently computes plain amortization), `student-loan-tax-deduction-calculator.html` (should compute the up-to-$2,500 student loan interest deduction phaseout per IRC §221, currently computes plain amortization). Math is technically correct *for what it computes* but the page promises different functionality. These three are content/UX bugs, not math bugs. **NOT audited this run** — needs human review or a future morning slot to redesign each correctly.
  - **Two `payday-loan-calculator.html` / `title-loan-calculator.html` files identified as similar mislabel pattern.** Both share the SAME implementation as `debt-consolidation-loan-calculator.html` — all three take Total Debt / Monthly Income / Monthly Expenses inputs and compute disposable income + DTI ratio. None of them compute payday-loan APR (typical 391-664% APR per CFPB), title-loan APR (typical 25%/month = 300% APR), or debt-consolidation comparison (consolidated rate vs weighted-average original rate). The underlying disposable-income / DTI math is correct, but all three are mislabeled. **NOT audited this run** — flag for future redesign (each needs its own correct formula).
  - **The 4.228% PLUS origination fee is small but real.** On a $35K PLUS loan, 4.228% = $1,480 deducted at disbursement. The borrower receives $33,520 net but owes $35,000 + interest. The amortization math is correct for the gross balance, but the borrower's *effective cost* is higher than the displayed total. Flag for UX refresh — add an "Origination fee" input field that subtracts from disbursed amount but keeps balance for amortization.
  - **The student-loan-refinance PSLF/IDR forfeiture caveat is the highest-leverage editorial-responsibility item in this batch.** Refinancing federal loans into private refi is irreversible — borrowers cannot un-refi back into the federal system. CalcLeap's competitor finance press routinely shows lower-rate refi quotes without surfacing the forfeiture — adding this warning would distinguish CalcLeap on the AdSense quality-review dimension (same E-E-A-T story as the 2026-06-08 morning HELOC piece's Rodriguez case-study behavioral caveat).

Its formula at `app.js:871` is identical to the 7 audited files (`monthly = loan * (rate * Math.pow(1 + rate, term)) / (Math.pow(1 + rate, term) - 1)`), so the math is already correct, but adding an AUDIT comment to the `app.js` function (or to the HTML page) is a separate task — held for a future run since `app.js` is a shared library and a comment there documents the rendering for several pages at once.   - Three case studies, each chosen to surface a different structural decision point:
    - **Maya (Pittsburgh, $58K income, $8K across 3 cards, fragile motivation)** — small-debt-load case where the smallest balance (store card $1,200 at 29.99%) is also the highest APR. Snowball and avalanche agree on identical priority order: Store → Card #1 → Card #2 → debt-free in 25 months with $1,540 total interest. The lesson: when the methods agree, the question is over.
    - **The Patels (combined $142K income, $25K across 5 cards, stable budget)** — mid-debt-load case showing the methods converge in many realistic portfolios (because high-APR store/gas cards tend to be small balances). Re-run with Card C and E swapped to force divergence shows avalanche saves $1,710 vs snowball; hybrid ties avalanche because the first snowball target was already the highest APR.
    - **The Reillys ($95K single income, $50K across 4 debts including a $1,800 settled medical bill at 0% effective APR)** — large-debt-load case where the methods diverge dramatically. Pure snowball costs $2,800 more than avalanche; hybrid (medical first to capture month-7 win, then avalanche) ties avalanche within $240 of total interest. The hybrid is the strict expected-value optimum when behavior risk is real. Includes the 1099-C tax-on-forgiven-debt callout per IRS Topic 431.
  - **Six mistakes section:** (1) paying the minimum and calling it progress; (2) using same-bank savings (bank offset risk); (3) closing paid-off cards (utilization ratio drop hurts credit score); (4) agonizing over method choice when methods agree; (5) hiring a debt settlement company (15-25% fee + credit-score crater + Form 1099-C tax liability); (6) skipping the $1,000-$2,000 starter emergency buffer.

  - **The behavioral-research register is the unique value-add over competitor finance press.** Most popular finance press treats the snowball-vs-avalanche choice as either Dave-Ramsey-style behavioral advocacy or NerdWallet-style "avalanche always saves more money" — neither cites the underlying peer-reviewed research. The piece names both the Kettle/Trudel/Blanchard/Häubl 2016 JCR study and the Gal/McShane 2012 JMR study with publication-grade citations, frames the snowball's win as an empirically-measured completion-probability effect rather than a folk-wisdom assertion, and computes the expected-value break-even where the behavioral premium pays for itself. This is the kind of citation density AdSense quality reviewers look for — the same E-E-A-T register that distinguished the 2026-06-08 HELOC piece's CFPB-research caveat on the debt-consolidation Rodriguez case.
  - **The hybrid method gets a full section, not a footnote.** Most snowball-vs-avalanche pieces treat hybrid as a curiosity. The piece argues — and the Reillys case study demonstrates — that the hybrid is the strict expected-value optimum for most real consumer portfolios because the first one or two snowball targets are by construction small, so the avalanche kicks in by month 6-10 and runs for the bulk of the payoff timeline. The piece names this pattern explicitly with quantitative rules: "smallest balance under 5% of total → run hybrid; smallest balance also lowest APR and >25% of total → skip hybrid, pure avalanche."
  - **The minimum-payment trap section is load-bearing.** The fact that a 2% minimum payment on a 21.52% APR keeps a $5,000 balance alive for 24+ years and produces total interest >100% of the original balance is the editorial hook that connects the piece to the AdSense "low value content" rejection. Most consumer finance press shows the calculator output but doesn't decompose the math — the piece walks the formula `min = max(floor, balance × 2% + interest_accrued)` and demonstrates that the minimum payment is the lender's optimum, not the borrower's. This is exactly the educational depth that AdSense quality reviewers cite as the gap between rejection and approval.
  - **The 1099-C tax-on-forgiven-debt callout in the Reillys case study.** Settled debt over $600 is taxable as ordinary income via Form 1099-C — a fact most consumer-debt content does not surface even when discussing settled debts. Adding this fact distinguishes CalcLeap on the editorial-responsibility dimension, same E-E-A-T register as the HELOC piece's CFPB reload-behavior caveat. Also surfaces the FTC Telemarketing Sales Rule §310.4(a)(5) advance-fee ban on debt relief services — useful counter-context for readers being marketed to by debt-settlement firms.
  - **Cross-links from the HELOC piece's Rodriguez case study.** The 2026-06-08 HELOC piece's "convert unsecured to secured debt" warning in the Rodriguez (debt-consolidation) case study naturally hands off to today's piece as the next step in the reading path — and today's "When consolidation makes the question moot" section explicitly references and links the HELOC piece. The two-piece cluster now reads as a coherent debt-strategy editorial unit.
  - **Calculator-target deviation from the 2026-06-08 evening recommendation.** The prior run recommended anchoring on `/calc/debt-payoff-calculator.html` and `/calc/credit-card-payoff-calculator.html`, neither of which exists in `/calc/`. The actual calculators are root-level (`/debt-payoff-calculator.html`, `/credit-card-payoff-calculator.html`, `/debt-snowball-calculator.html`, `/debt-avalanche-calculator.html`, `/debt-consolidation-calculator.html`) and `/calc/credit-card-payoff.html` + `/calc/debt-to-income.html`. The new piece links the correct paths. This surfaces a TIER 4 flag: the debt-calculator inventory is split between root and `/calc/` directories, which is structurally inconsistent with the home-finance cluster's `/calc/heloc-calculator.html` / `/calc/home-equity-loan-calculator.html` layout. Cleaning up the layout is a future architectural decision but not urgent.

The APR approximation is `(totalInterest / (amount/2) / years) * 100` which neglects the fee impact on effective amount financed. - **Canonical formula (identical across 6 of 7 files; 1 file differs):**
  - **Standard amortization (6 files: car, personal, mortgage-payment, loan-comparison, HEL, refi):** `monthlyPayment = P * r * (1+r)^n / ((1+r)^n - 1)` where `r = annualRate/100/12`. Input unit for `n` varies by file: car/personal/mortgage-payment use n in MONTHS directly (term select stores values like `60`, `36`, `360`); HEL/loan-comparison/refi use n in YEARS multiplied *12 internally.
  - **Time-to-payoff iterator (1 file: student-loan):** different family — solves for MONTHS given fixed monthly payment via `while(b>0 && months<600) { months++; intr=b*r; totalInt+=intr; b=max(0, b-(mp-intr)); }`. Math is correct; edge case is `mp <= b*r` (payment never exceeds interest accrual) which runs to the 600-month cap silently — flag for UX warning.
  - **Zero-rate branch:** ABSENT in all 7 files. Not a defect at default inputs but would produce NaN if a user enters rate=0.

  - **Mortgage payment PITI:** no zero-rate branch; PMI hardcoded at 0.5% annual (CFPB/HPA range 0.20-2.25%); does NOT model HPA auto-termination at 78% LTV (PMI continues throughout displayed life of loan); no HOA dues, flood insurance, or tax/insurance escalation modeled; 6.5% default is 14bps above Freddie Mac PMMS week-ending 2026-05-14 (30-yr 6.36%).

This is a UX bug (math correct, decision logic incomplete) — flag for future fix where the "favorable" tier requires both `breakEvenMonths ≤ 36` AND `netLifeSavings > 0`. The TC1+TC2 cases are the classic "term-extension trap" that consumer-finance press routinely warns about; the calculator surfaces the netSavings figure but the recommendation banner is the user's first-glance signal, and it currently doesn't reflect it.

- **Notable observations:**
  - **The mortgage-refinance "term-extension trap" is the highest-leverage UX bug surfaced in this batch.** The math is correct (it computes netLifeSavings correctly and displays the figure) but the recommendation banner ("Refinancing looks favorable!" / "Consider your timeline" / "Long break-even period") branches ONLY on breakEvenMonths and monthlySavings, never on netLifeSavings. So for the TC1 scenario ($250K bal, 6.5%/25y → 5.5%/30y, $5K closing) the calculator says "Refinancing looks favorable! You'll save $268.55/mo and break even in 1.55y" — while simultaneously showing netLifeSavings=-$9,605. The display row labeled "Lifetime Savings" reflects the truth but the user's first-glance signal contradicts it. This is exactly the kind of misleading calculator output that AdSense quality reviewers cite as a "low value content" signal. Fix is small (add `&& netSavings > 0` to the favorable-tier branches), but should be done in a focused commit with new test cases — held for a future evening that has time for tier-logic redesign + visual review.
  - **The loan-comparison APR approximation understates TILA APR by ~45-50 bps in the typical-fee case.** The simple-interest formula `(totalInterest / (amount/2) / years) * 100` is a tolerable midpoint estimate when fees are zero but understates significantly with non-zero fees because the formula treats `amount` as the financing base when the borrower actually receives `amount - fees`. For TC1 ($20K @ 7%/5y + $500 fees), Python iterative TILA solver gives ~7.97% vs the displayed 7.52% — 45bps gap. For high-fee loans (origination fees 5%+) the gap widens to 100+ bps. This is exactly the metric consumers compare across lenders, so an understated APR is the highest-impact correctness issue in this batch. Held for a future evening — replacement requires a Newton iteration or bisection solver in JS.
  - **The student-loan rate default 5.5% is now stale across BOTH the root file (audited 2026-06-08) AND the /calc/ file.** The federal rate for new disbursements (AY2025-26) is 6.53% (Undergrad Direct), 8.08% (Grad Unsub), 9.08% (PLUS). The 5.5% default matches AY2020-21 era. A user computing payoff timeline with 5.5% will underestimate total interest by ~25-40% on a $35K loan. This is a quick fix (single character change in the input default) but should be done in a coordinated batch across all 4 student-loan files (root /student-loan-calculator.html doesn't exist standalone but the 3 mislabeled student-loan files + /calc/student-loan-calculator.html share the default).
  - **The PMI hardcoded at 0.5% is a midpoint not a worst-case.** Borrowers with 5% down + 660 FICO can see PMI of 1.5-2.25% — meaning the calculator understates monthly PMI by ~$300/mo on a $400K home. Borrowers with 15% down + 760+ FICO see PMI of 0.25-0.35% — overstating by ~$50/mo. The midpoint is fine as a default but the calculator should expose PMI rate as an input. Held for future UX refresh.
  - **The /calc/ HEL file is a /calc/-flavored clone of the root HEL file with one distinction:** the /calc/ file ADDS the LTV/CLTV warning logic (`cltv>85` rejected, `80<cltv≤85` cautioned) and the "available equity" computation, neither of which appears in the root file audited 2026-06-08. The /calc/ file is the more capable HEL calculator; the root file is the simpler product. Worth noting for future content cross-link decisions.
  - **All 7 single-line-minified scripts accepted the block-comment injection without parse errors.** The minifier preserves the `/* ... */` block comment boundaries exactly. Verified via `new Function(body)` for all 7. The injection script (`/tmp/inject_audits.py`) uses idempotency check `"AUDIT 2026-06-09" in src` so a re-run on the same files is a no-op.

Equivalent whole-life cash value at year 30 ~$250-400K depending on carrier and dividend performance.

  - Three case studies, each surfacing a different structural decision point:
    - **Jamie + Casey (30yo dual-income parents, $1.5M DIME need, 20-year window)** — small-children-and-mortgage case where 20-year term dominates by ~2× on the buy-term-and-invest-the-difference math. Combined term premium $94/mo vs combined whole-life equivalent $1,400/mo; $1,306/mo difference at 8% over 20 years = $769K. The lesson: when the need has an end date, term wins decisively.
    - **Marcus (52yo $13.8M-net-worth business owner with $4.5M estate-tax overage)** — high-net-worth estate-planning case where a $2M whole life policy held inside an ILIT provides the liquidity to pay 40% federal estate tax without forcing a fire-sale of an illiquid business asset. Premium $4,500/mo funded by Crummey-noticed gifts within the $19K 2026 annual exclusion. The lesson: when the death-benefit need is permanent and the alternative is forced asset liquidation, whole life is the right tool.
    - **Lin + Wei (parents of child with Down syndrome, $1.2M permanent need for SNT funding)** — special-needs case where a $620/mo survivorship (second-to-die) whole life policy funds a Special Needs Trust without disqualifying the child from Medicaid/SSI means-tested benefits. The lesson: when the support need does not end with the parent's "normal" insurance window, term is structurally inadequate.
  - **Seven mistakes section:** (1) treating employer coverage as enough; (2) buying whole life when need is temporary; (3) delaying the purchase (8-12% rate increase per year of delay); (4) forgetting the conversion rider; (5) naming a minor child as direct beneficiary; (6) letting policy lapse during cash-flow crunch; (7) buying based on agent's recommendation alone (captive vs independent vs fee-only planner distinction).

- **Notable observations:**
  - **First insurance-cluster gold-standard piece in the library.** All prior 23 gold-standard pieces sit in the home-finance, taxes, retirement, BMI/health, or developer-tools clusters. The insurance cluster has been the structural gap — CalcLeap operates 60+ insurance calculators (auto, home, health, life, LTC, disability, pet, boat, RV, umbrella, motorcycle, business, flood, earthquake, plus 50-state life insurance pages) but had zero gold-standard editorial coverage of any insurance topic until today. Term-vs-whole was the highest-traffic-potential entry point because the question is universal (every working adult with dependents asks it) and the answer is non-obvious (the agent-channel default is whole-life upsell, the fee-only-planner default is buy-term, and most consumers do not know there's a meaningful debate). The piece opens a structural lane for follow-up insurance gold-standard pieces — natural successors include "How much life insurance do I need?" (DIME-focused), "Long-term care insurance: is it worth it?", "Disability insurance: short-term vs long-term", and "Umbrella liability insurance: the math at $1M-$5M coverage."
  - **Third direct head-to-head comparison piece, consolidating the structural pattern.** The 12-row side-by-side dimension table (compressed here from the 15-row HELOC pattern + 10-row debt method pattern) is now confirmed as the reusable structure for any binary-decision question in the gold-standard library. The pattern handles the reader's actual decision-arrival framing — "I'm choosing between X and Y" — better than the deep-dive single-topic structure. Natural future applications: "Roth conversion vs traditional contribution" (TIER 2 follow-up), "Lease vs buy a car" (TIER 2), "Rent vs buy a home" (already partially covered by `how-much-house-can-i-afford` but the head-to-head framing would add a new angle).
  - **The 8,463-word length is the highest in the library.** Prior leader was `self-employed-taxes-1099-survival-guide-2026` at 7,278 words. The depth was structurally required: the topic spans four distinct evidentiary registers (actuarial mortality, 2026 dividend rate environment, future-value math, narrow whole-life use cases) plus four hybrid-product subtopics (IUL, VUL, ROP, survivorship) plus DIME sizing plus shopping mechanics plus seven mistakes plus taxes. Cutting any of those would have left a meaningful question unanswered for the reader. The piece sits comfortably within the AdSense "depth beyond the headline question" register that the program is targeting.
  - **The "dividend rate is not your return" callout is the highest-leverage editorial-responsibility item.** Most consumer-finance press treats the 6.60%/6.40%/5.75% dividend interest rate as if it were a direct return on premium dollars — which is a meaningful misrepresentation. The callout explains that the dividend is credited against guaranteed insurance costs and expense charges, not paid directly as a return on premium, and that the realized after-cost IRR on a 30-year hold typically lands at 3-5%. This is exactly the kind of specific actionable arithmetic that earns E-E-A-T credit and distinguishes CalcLeap from the agent-channel-funded content that dominates the search results for these queries.
  - **The Marcus (estate-tax) and Lin+Wei (special-needs) case studies deliberately argue FOR whole life.** Most fee-only-planner content treats whole life as universally inferior — a common rhetorical overreach. The piece is more careful: it walks the specific actuarial and structural reasons whole life wins for the estate-tax-overage case (illiquid business asset, 40% federal estate-tax rate above exemption, ILIT structure with Crummey gifting) and the special-needs case (permanent support need that does not age out, SNT funding requirement, means-tested benefits coordination). This nuanced framing distinguishes CalcLeap on editorial-responsibility — the same register that distinguished the HELOC piece's Rodriguez case-study warning and the debt-snowball piece's Reillys 1099-C tax callout. AdSense quality reviewers explicitly look for content that resists both extremes (universal recommendation, universal rejection) in favor of accurate use-case scoping.
  - **The Roth IRA buy-term-and-invest-the-difference handoff opens a natural cross-cluster bridge.** The Jamie+Casey case study explicitly walks the reader from term-life-insurance into Roth IRA / index-fund investing, and the calculator sidebar surfaces `/calc/roth-ira-calculator.html` and `/calc/compound-interest.html`. This is the first gold-standard piece in the library to bridge from the insurance cluster into the retirement-investment cluster. Inbound traffic from this piece should naturally feed traffic to the 2026-06-02 `how-much-do-i-need-to-retire` piece and the 2026-06-03 `roth-ira-vs-traditional-ira-2026` piece, both linked in the related-reading sidebar.
  - **Calculator-target deviation from the 2026-06-09 evening recommendation.** The prior run recommended anchoring on `/term-life-insurance-calculator.html`, `/whole-life-insurance-calculator.html`, and `/life-insurance-calculator.html` — but only the third file actually exists in the repo. Only the single DIME-needs `/life-insurance-calculator.html` (root-level) and the 50 per-state `/life-insurance/{state}-life-insurance-calculator.html` files exist. The piece links the correct paths. This surfaces a TIER 3/4 flag: CalcLeap does not currently operate dedicated term-only or whole-life-only calculator products — every life-insurance calculator entry point is the DIME-needs sizing tool plus state-specific rate references. A future TIER 3 morning could build dedicated term-rate-by-age and whole-life-premium-by-age calculators to capture the long-tail of users coming directly into a comparison query rather than a needs-assessment query.

Second pass removed the new duplicate visible block, extracted the 8 existing Q&A pairs via regex from `<div class="faq-question">` / `<div class="faq-answer">` siblings, rebuilt the FAQPage schema from those exact strings, and added `id="faq"` to the existing `<div class="faq">` so screen readers + sitemaps can target it. Both files now have 1 visible FAQ section (8 items) with a matching 8-question FAQPage schema, exactly per Google's structured-data alignment requirements.

Topics are pulled from the AUDIT comments' "known simplifications / flags" sections so the FAQ surfaces the same caveats the AUDIT documented.

Four-year build at $533/month (4.7% of take-home). The lesson: pick a single account, automate, hit target in 3-5 years.
    - **Jordan (41, single-earner, retail-management layoff in March)** — exact-math case showing how an 8-month fund ($38,400) combined with 6 weeks severance ($9,400 net) + 26-week state UI ($16,120) covered a 7-month income gap with only $8,100 of fund drawdown. The lesson: the math during the emergency is downstream of the math before it; Jordan's prior contribution discipline turned catastrophe into annoyance.
    - **Pat + Robin (68, retired, $1.6M portfolio, $9,800/mo essentials)** — sequence-of-returns case showing how 18 months of essentials ($176,400) split across HYSA + 13-week T-bill ladder lets retirees pause portfolio withdrawals during equity drawdowns. Direct tie to Bengen 4%-rule literature. The lesson: retirees need MORE cash buffer, not less; the cash is what makes the 4% rule survive contact with reality.
  - **Five-step ladder** (Rung 1 open the account / Rung 2 starter $1-2k / Rung 3 kill high-interest debt / Rung 4 fully fund the reserve / Rung 5 maintain and don't oversave) — gives the reader a concrete progression from zero to fully funded, with timing estimates for each rung.
  - **Seven mistakes section:** (1) keeping it in checking [$1,020/yr foregone yield example]; (2) sizing by income instead of essentials; (3) investing the fund "to make it work harder"; (4) one account for emergency + sinking funds; (5) treating it as sunk cost (misses 4% cash yield); (6) replacing with credit card "in case of emergency"; (7) never recalibrating.

### 2026-06-11 — Evening (Claude, evening routine) — TIER 3 UX-bug fix on mortgage-refinance pair
- **Item:** Picked option (a) from the 2026-06-09 evening "Next" queue (re-affirmed by 2026-06-10 evening and 2026-06-11 morning) — fix the mortgage-refinance recommendation-tier UX bug across both `/calc/mortgage-refinance-calculator.html` and the root `mortgage-refinance-calculator.html`. The 2026-06-09 audit had documented that recommendation-tier logic branched only on `monthlySavings + breakEvenMonths`, never on `netSavings` / `lifetimeSavings`, so the calculator could simultaneously display "Refinancing looks favorable!" AND a negative lifetime-savings figure. This is exactly the misleading-output pattern AdSense quality reviewers cite as a "low value content" signal — the user's first-glance recommendation banner contradicting the truth shown one row below it.
- **Bug confirmed in both files (pre-fix):**
  - `/calc/mortgage-refinance-calculator.html` lines 690-700: 4-tier ladder branching on `monthlySavings > 0` × `breakEvenYears` buckets (≤3y favorable / 3-7y consider / >7y long / else not-saving). `netSavings` was computed and displayed in a separate row but never entered the recommendation logic. **TC1** ($250K, 6.5%/25y → 5.5%/30y, $5K closing) hit the "favorable" branch with `breakEvenYears=1.55` despite `netSavings=-$9,605`. **TC2** ($300K, 7.0%/20y → 6.0%/30y, $6K) hit "favorable" with `breakEvenYears=0.95` despite `netSavings=-$95,299` (the classic term-extension trap).
  - Root `/mortgage-refinance-calculator.html` lines 423-443: simpler 2-branch (green ✅ if `monthlySavings > 0` else red ❌). `lifetimeSavings` was COMPUTED on line 428 but was NEVER DISPLAYED to the user AND never entered the verdict logic. So the root file had a strictly worse version of the same bug — the negative lifetime savings was both invisible AND ignored.
- **Fix in `/calc/mortgage-refinance-calculator.html`:**
  - Added a new highest-priority branch BEFORE the existing 4-tier ladder: `if (monthlySavings > 0 && netSavings <= 0)` → renders a `"⚠ Term-extension trap."` warning that surfaces both the monthly drop AND the lifetime-cost increase, with concrete copy explaining "Lower monthly payments are not the same as saving money" and suggesting three remediation paths (keep current term, make extra principal payments, refinance into a term matching remaining years).
  - Added `&& netSavings > 0` to each of the three `monthlySavings > 0` tier conditions (favorable / consider-timeline / long-breakeven) — defense-in-depth so a future re-ordering of the branches cannot re-introduce the bug.
  - Enhanced the "consider your timeline" and "long break-even period" copy to surface the netSavings figure explicitly inline ("net $X over the life of the loan") so the user sees the lifetime number in the same sentence as the timeline tradeoff.
  - Added a 4-line block-comment FIX 2026-06-11 marker explaining the change for future maintainers, referencing AUDIT 2026-06-09 TC1/TC2.
- **Fix in root `/mortgage-refinance-calculator.html`:**
  - Added a new `result-detail` line displaying "Lifetime Savings (after closing costs): $X" or "-$X" — the previously-computed-but-hidden `lifetimeSavings` figure is now visible.
  - Replaced the 2-branch verdict with a 3-branch: green ✅ if BOTH `monthlySavings > 0 AND lifetimeSavings > 0` (now shows both the monthly figure AND the lifetime figure in one sentence — "saves you $X/month and $Y over the life of the loan"); amber ⚠️ "Term-extension trap" if `monthlySavings > 0 && lifetimeSavings <= 0` with concrete copy showing the dollar amount the user would actually pay MORE; red ❌ if monthly increases.
  - Added a 3-line block-comment FIX 2026-06-11 marker.
  - Added a full AUDIT 2026-06-11 block comment (24 lines) at the top of the `<script>` block — same template as the 2026-06-08 / 2026-06-09 batches — documenting formula, the 3 test cases (TC1 lifetimeSavings=-$9,605, TC2=-$95,299, TC3=+$9,849 — exact-bit match to the /calc/ sibling audited 2026-06-09), and 4 known flags (no zero-rate branch, simple break-even ignores opportunity cost, prior version hid lifetimeSavings, no cash-out vs rate-and-term distinction). This bumps the root file from un-audited into the verified-math cluster as a side benefit of the UX fix — the math verification was free given the test cases were already hand-derived for the /calc/ sibling.
- **Math invariance verified by live JS execution.** Both files' `function calculate()` bodies were extracted via regex, fed to `new Function(body)` against a stubbed `document.getElementById` returning each TC's inputs, and the captured DOM outputs were compared to the 2026-06-09 AUDIT comment's expected values:
  - `/calc/` TC1: monthlySavings=$268.55 ✓ · breakEven=1.6y ✓ · netSavings=-$9,605 ✓ · verdict=TERM_EXTENSION_TRAP ✓
  - `/calc/` TC2: monthlySavings=$527.25 ✓ · netSavings=-$95,299 ✓ · verdict=TERM_EXTENSION_TRAP ✓
  - `/calc/` TC3: monthlySavings=$535.53 ✓ · netSavings=+$9,849 ✓ · verdict=FAVORABLE ✓
  - Root TC1: monthlySavings=$268.55 ✓ · lifetimeSavings=-$9,605 ✓ · verdict=TRAP ✓
  - Root TC2: lifetimeSavings=-$95,299 ✓ · verdict=TRAP ✓
  - Root TC3: lifetimeSavings=+$9,849 ✓ · verdict=GREEN ✓
  - Edge cases via simulation: `monthlySave>0, breakEven=5y, netSave=+$15K` → CONSIDER_TIMELINE; `monthlySave>0, breakEven=9y, netSave=+$5K` → LONG_BREAKEVEN; `monthlySave<0` → NOT_SAVING (red banner unchanged).

The mortgage-refinance UX bug — the highest-leverage AdSense-quality signal flagged in the 2026-06-09 evening batch and re-flagged twice — is now resolved on both files in the pair. Loan-cluster verified-math coverage now spans 15 files (7 root from 2026-06-08 + 7 /calc/ from 2026-06-09 + 1 root mortgage-refinance from tonight).
- **Notable observations:**
  - **The root file's bug was strictly worse than the /calc/ file's bug.** The /calc/ file had wrong-but-displayed recommendation logic (user could at least see the negative netSavings in a separate row and reconcile the contradiction). The root file had wrong-recommendation-logic AND hid the lifetimeSavings entirely (user had no way to see the term-extension trap even by careful reading — the figure was computed only to be discarded into an unused variable). The root file's fix therefore delivers more SEO/quality signal than the /calc/ fix because previously-invisible information is now surfaced.
  - **Re-using the 2026-06-09 AUDIT test cases for the root file was a 0-cost verification win.** The root file uses identical math (canonical amortization × 2 + break-even + lifetime delta), only with different DOM ids (`current-balance` vs `currentBalance`) and slightly different rounding behavior (ceil on breakEvenMonths). I confirmed exact-bit match to the 2026-06-09 AUDIT TC1/TC2/TC3 lifetimeSavings values without re-deriving from scratch. This is the kind of cluster-level efficiency the verified-math program is supposed to extract — once one file in a family is audited, sibling files in the same family can be batch-verified by execution.
  - **The "term-extension trap" copy is calibrated to consumer-finance press register.** "Lower monthly payments are not the same as saving money" is the exact framing NerdWallet / Investopedia / Bankrate use when explaining this concept. Surfacing it in a calculator's primary recommendation banner — at the moment the user is about to make the decision — is more impactful than burying it in a blog post; the calculator IS the user's moment of decision. This is exactly the kind of editorial-responsibility surface that AdSense quality reviewers credit as "depth beyond the headline number."
  - **The fix is defensive at TWO levels.** The new highest-priority `monthlySavings > 0 && netSavings <= 0` branch catches the trap. Even if a future refactor re-orders the branches, the `&& netSavings > 0` condition added to each subsequent tier means the trap cannot leak into a positive verdict. Belt + suspenders.
  - **3 of the 4 monthly-savings>0 branches now reference netSavings in their displayed copy.** Even before the user reads the lifetime row, the recommendation banner itself references the lifetime number — reducing the cognitive distance between "monthly saves $X" and "lifetime nets $Y" to zero characters. This matters because most users only read the first banner and stop.
  - **No `og-default.png` regeneration this run.** Open question carried forward from 2026-05-31 evening flag.
  - **Calculator design decision held for future runs:** the recommendation banner could also surface the term-extension trap when the user has chosen `newTerm` greater than `currentYears` (i.e., the trap-inducing input pattern) BEFORE clicking calculate — a "term mismatch warning" inline-validation pattern.   - Three case studies, each surfacing a different structural decision point:
    - **Priya + Marcus (33/35, Brooklyn dual-income $185K, first baby, unplanned cesarean)** — high-cost-metro household with strong leave benefits and full DCFSA election. Year-one cash impact ~$48,500 after CTC and Empire State Child Credit. The lesson: even at $185K combined income, the year is large; the household absorbed it because they walked in with $30K cushion + $25K emergency fund. Pre-baby preparation is the deciding variable, not income.
    - **Hannah + Jason (37/38, rural Indiana, third baby, single-income on HDHP-HSA, no state PFL, family caregiving)** — low-cost-state household with HSA pre-funded for years exactly for this case. Year-one cash impact ~$10,000 after CTC × 3 (with refundable ACTC), WIC, and family childcare. The lesson: HSAs are designed exactly for known major medical events; family caregiving zeroes out the childcare line; WIC is real money for eligible households.
    - **Olivia + Sam (30/32, suburban Denver, twin birth, $148K dual-income, no employer paid leave for her, CO FAMLI)** — the case study where preparation was inadequate. $18K savings going in vs ~$57,500 first-year net impact. Closed the gap with a $10K HELOC at 8.4% and a 12-month pause on 401(k) above-match contributions costing ~$11K of 30-year future value. The lesson: under-preparation has a quantifiable long-tail cost beyond the immediate cash crunch.
  - **Five pre-baby financial moves section:** (1) emergency fund to 6 months of post-baby essentials; (2) right health-plan tier at next open enrollment with explicit attention to deductible/OOP/maternity coverage; (3) pre-fund HSA $8,550 or DCFSA $5,000 at open enrollment; (4) open 529 plan at birth — even $25 starts the compounding clock ($200/mo at 6% real → $77K by 18 vs $43K starting at age 5); (5) review life insurance + will + guardianship designation.
  - **Seven mistakes section:** (1) missing the 30/60-day insurance enrollment window; (2) skipping the DCFSA election; (3) buying premium gear when federal safety standards apply equally; (4) pausing 401(k) below the match (50-100% immediate return foregone); (5) carrying medical bills on a credit card without negotiating with hospital billing (30-50% settlement common); (6) not applying for SSN at hospital (delays CTC claim, insurance enrollment, 529 setup); (7) underestimating the leave income gap (the part most households don't see coming).

- **Notable observations:**
  - **First family/life-event piece in the library, opening a structurally new cluster.** All 25 prior gold-standard pieces sit in finance/retirement/taxes/insurance/health/developer-tools clusters. The family/life-event cluster has been a structural gap — CalcLeap operates `/calc/childbirth-cost-calculator.html`, `/college-savings-calculator.html`, `/ivf-cost-calculator.html` (verified at root via earlier audit), `/wedding-cost-calculator.html`, `/divorce-cost-calculator.html`, and adoption-cost related tools, all without gold-standard editorial coverage. Cost-of-having-a-baby is the highest-traffic-potential entry point because the question is searched roughly 200K+ times per month nationally and the answers in market are dominated by either calculator sites with thin content or parenting press without primary-source rigor on the tax/leave/insurance pieces. The piece opens a structural lane for follow-up family-cluster pieces — natural successors: "Cost of divorce in 2026" (already in TIER 2 #15), "Cost of fertility treatment" (IVF cycle ~$15-25K + meds), "Wedding cost calculator companion" (~$30K national average per The Knot), "Adoption costs" (private domestic ~$25-50K, international ~$25-70K, foster minimal).
  - **The piece's structural shape unifies six budgets into one framework — a reusable pattern.** The "six overlapping budgets" framing (prenatal / delivery / first-year / childcare / leave / one-time) breaks the topic into manageable pieces and is reusable for any major life-event piece. Same shape would handle "Cost of divorce" (legal fees / household split / custody costs / child support / housing reshuffle / health insurance separation), "Cost of starting a business" (legal/structure / equipment / first-year operating / personal income gap / health insurance / tax-prep complexity), or "Cost of caring for an aging parent" (housing modifications / in-home help / medical out-of-pocket / lost work hours / legal/financial planning / one-time setup).
  - **Each case study deliberately illustrates a different preparation regime.** The NYC household ($185K dual-income, prepared cushion, strong leave) absorbs the year without debt. The rural household ($74K single-income, HSA pre-funded, family caregiving, WIC eligible) absorbs it for $10K net via the systems designed exactly for them. The Denver twin household ($148K dual-income, under-prepared cushion, weak leave on her side) absorbs it with debt + paused retirement contributions. The triangulation — high-income unprepared can still struggle; lower-income prepared can still absorb — is the kind of nuanced register that distinguishes editorially-serious content from generic "raising a kid is expensive" listicles.
  - **The 26 inline source references is the densest citation pattern in the library.** Prior leader was the 2026-06-10 term-vs-whole-life piece at 26 references. The density was required because the piece spans four distinct evidentiary registers — medical cost data (Peterson-KFF, CDC, HHS), benefits/tax law (IRS, DOL, CMS, state agencies), childcare-economy research (Child Care Aware America, BLS), and long-term household economics (USDA, Brookings, College Board) — each requiring its own primary-source anchor. Without the citation density the piece would read like opinion; with it, it reads like a research brief. This is exactly the editorial register AdSense quality reviewers credit and the register competitor parenting press (BabyCenter, What to Expect, Parents.com) routinely fails to maintain.
  - **The CTC $2,200/$1,700 refundable figure is correct for tax year 2026 per OBBBA §70104.** Pre-OBBBA, the CTC was scheduled to revert from $2,000 to $1,000 in 2026 with the TCJA sunset. OBBBA (Public Law 119-21, signed July 4 2025) made the CTC permanent and raised it to $2,200 starting TY2025, indexed for inflation thereafter. The refundable Additional Child Tax Credit (ACTC) portion was raised from $1,600 to $1,700. The phaseout thresholds ($200K single / $400K MFJ) were retained from TCJA. This is the same statutory citation register the 2026-06-04 piece used for the federal-MFJ-bracket fix and the 2026-06-06 piece used for the HoH-bracket extension — internal consistency with prior gold-standard pieces' tax citations.
  - **The 13-state + DC paid-family-leave inventory is current to January 1, 2026.** California (1st, 2004), New Jersey (2009), Rhode Island (2014), New York (2018), Washington (2020), Massachusetts (2021), Connecticut (2022), Oregon (2023), Colorado (FAMLI, 2024), Maryland (2025), Delaware (2026), Minnesota (2026), Maine (ramping 2026), plus DC. The Delaware and Minnesota Jan 1 2026 launches were the most recent additions; both are documented at their state DOL/labor-department pages cited in the methodology footer. This is the first gold-standard piece to inventory the state PFL program landscape — opens a natural future TIER 3/4 item to build state-by-state PFL calculators surfacing the wage cap / replacement rate / duration per state.
  - **The piece's $310,000 lifetime cost figure is intentionally framed as a $1,440/month spread.** Most consumer-finance press cites the USDA $233K (or inflation-adjusted $310K) number as a single headline that scares readers and stops the conversation. The piece reframes it as a 216-month spread, then notes that most households are already spending most of the housing/food categories regardless of the child — so the marginal cost to an existing household after the childcare years drops to $400-$800/mo. The reframe is a deliberate AdSense-quality signal: it answers the question that the reader actually has (can I afford this?) more usefully than the alarmist single-number framing.
  - **`/calc/childbirth-cost-calculator.html` is the anchor calculator and was verified to exist via `ls` pre-flight** (file present, opens cleanly). This calculator is a structurally important entry point that has not been audited as part of any prior verified-math batch — it sits adjacent to the 2026-06-08/09/11 evening loan-mortgage audits and is the natural next non-loan calculator to audit. Surfacing it as the hero CTA on the highest-traffic-potential family-cluster piece in the library increases its surface area substantially and creates the right incentive to prioritize its math/UX audit.

### 2026-06-12 — Evening (Claude, evening routine) — TIER 3 UX refresh: stale student-loan rate defaults
- **Item:** Picked option (b) from the 2026-06-09 / 2026-06-10 / 2026-06-11 evening "Next" queue (re-affirmed by 2026-06-12 morning) — refresh stale student-loan rate defaults from 5.5% to current AY2025-26 federal rates across the 4 files flagged in prior AUDIT comments: `/calc/student-loan-calculator.html`, `/student-loan-refinance-calculator.html`, `/grad-plus-loan-calculator.html`, `/parent-plus-loan-calculator.html`. Closes a 4-evening-old staleness flag carried forward from the 2026-06-08 / 2026-06-09 loan-cluster audits. The 5.5% defaults matched the AY2020-21 era — by AY2025-26 statutory rates per Dept of Ed (Bipartisan Student Loan Certainty Act 2013 formula = 10-yr T-Note May high yield + statutory margin), undergraduate Direct loans are 6.53%, graduate Direct Unsubsidized are 8.08%, and Direct PLUS (Grad + Parent) are 9.08%. A user loading any of these calculators would be off by 100-360 basis points from the rate their actual federal loan would carry — meaningfully wrong for the median borrower's planning use case.
- **Pre-flight inventory:** ran `grep -n 'value="5.5"' on all 4 files. Confirmed each has exactly one `id="rate" value="5.5"` input. Additionally identified that the 3 root files (refi, grad-plus, parent-plus) ALL have a hardcoded JS fallback `parseFloat(...) / 100 || 0.055` that would silently restore 5.5% if the user cleared the input — must be updated in lockstep with the UI default or the fix is incomplete. The /calc/ file uses unary-plus coercion (`+document.getElementById('rate').value/100/12`) which yields 0 for empty input (no fallback to update). Also identified that all 3 root files share an identical stale hint `<small>Federal loans: 4-7%, Private loans: 3-12%</small>` that is doubly wrong: federal loans for AY2025-26 are actually 6.53-9.08%, and the file-specific context (Grad PLUS / Parent PLUS) calls for a calculator-specific rate citation rather than a generic federal/private band.
- **What changed in each file (3 changes per file, 12 changes total across 4 files):**
  - **`/calc/student-loan-calculator.html`** (the time-to-payoff simulator): (a) UI default `value="5.5"` → `value="6.53"` on the `id="rate"` input — the most common borrower scenario is an undergraduate Direct loan at the AY2025-26 statutory rate; (b) added a 6-line FIX 2026-06-12 marker to the AUDIT block explaining the change, noting TC1 remains anchored at 5.5% as the historical hand-verified canonical test (TC values are formula inputs, not derived from UI defaults — the regression test is unchanged), and clarifying that FAQ wording using 5.5% as an illustrative example is intentionally preserved because it is an educational worked example, not a current-rate claim; (c) no JS fallback to update.
  - **`/student-loan-refinance-calculator.html`** (the private refi payment calculator): (a) UI default `value="5.5"` → `value="6.53"` — a mid-market 2026 private refi quote per the file's own FAQ ("Competitive 2026 fixed-rate offerings...span roughly 5.0%–9.0%"); 5.5% was at the low end (top-tier credit only); 6.53% is the median refi shopper's likely quote AND coincidentally matches the federal Undergrad Direct rate (a natural decision anchor — refi makes sense when the new rate is meaningfully below what the federal alternative would carry); (b) JS fallback `|| 0.055` → `|| 0.0653` so the calculator does not silently revert to the stale rate on empty input; (c) hint text `Federal loans: 4-7%, Private loans: 3-12%` → `Federal AY2025-26: 6.53% (Undergrad), 8.08% (Grad), 9.08% (PLUS) per Dept of Ed; private refi 2026: ~5-9% fixed` (current band, useful as a decision frame for refi shoppers comparing federal vs private); (d) added FIX 2026-06-12 marker to AUDIT block noting the change and noting that the "CRITICAL CONSUMER CAVEAT" about federal-to-private refi PSLF/IDR forfeiture is now partially mitigated by the 2026-06-10 evening FAQ schema injection (Q2 of the FAQPage explicitly warns) — but an inline UI warning remains held for a future UX evening.
  - **`/grad-plus-loan-calculator.html`**: (a) UI default `value="5.5"` → `value="9.08"` to match the AY2025-26 Grad PLUS statutory rate per Dept of Ed (Bipartisan Student Loan Certainty Act 2013 = 10-yr T-Note May high yield + 4.60%, capped at 10.50%); (b) JS fallback `|| 0.055` → `|| 0.0908`; (c) hint text replaced with a calculator-specific citation: `AY2025-26 Grad PLUS fixed rate: 9.08% per Dept of Ed (Bipartisan Student Loan Certainty Act 2013: 10-yr T-Note May high yield + 4.60%, capped at 10.50%)` — the hint now doubles as a mini-explainer of how the rate is set; (d) added FIX 2026-06-12 marker to AUDIT block, removed the now-resolved stale-default flag from the original 2026-06-08 audit's known-simplifications list.
  - **`/parent-plus-loan-calculator.html`**: same pattern as grad-plus — (a) UI default `value="5.5"` → `value="9.08"`; (b) JS fallback `|| 0.055` → `|| 0.0908`; (c) hint text refreshed to `AY2025-26 Parent PLUS fixed rate: 9.08% per Dept of Ed (Bipartisan Student Loan Certainty Act 2013: 10-yr T-Note May high yield + 4.60%, capped at 10.50%)`; (d) added FIX 2026-06-12 marker, removed the now-resolved stale-default flag.
- **Math invariance verified by live JS execution.** All three root files use IDENTICAL standard-amortization math (`M = P × r × (1+r)^n / ((1+r)^n − 1)`) — only differ in flavor copy and hint text — so a single re-implementation against the AUDIT TC1/TC2/TC3 values exercises all three. Ran the function body in Node against TC1 ($50K @ 7.0%/10y), TC2 ($80K @ 8.05%/10y), TC3 ($30K @ 6.5%/20y) — all three produced exact-bit match to the 2026-06-08 AUDIT expected values: TC1 monthly=$580.54, totalPaid=$69,665.09, totalInt=$19,665.09 ✓; TC2 $972.74 / $116,728.28 / $36,728.28 ✓; TC3 $223.67 / $53,681.27 / $23,681.27 ✓. Also separately verified the `/calc/student-loan-calculator.html` time-to-payoff simulator math against TC1 (bal=$35K, rate=5.5%, pay=$400 — anchored at the historical canonical rate, NOT the new UI default) — produced exact-bit match: 113 months = 9y 5m, totalInt = $9,835.30. Fallback verification: empty-input `parseFloat('') / 100 || 0.0653` → 0.0653 and `|| 0.0908` → 0.0908, both as expected. The rate-default refresh is purely a UI/default change; computed values for any user-entered rate are unchanged.
