The relevant signal is that the student-loan cluster's stale-default flag (open since 2026-06-08) is now closed: 4 of the 4 calculators in the student-loan family now load with their AY2025-26 statutory rate. The student-loan-forgiveness, student-loan-snowball, and student-loan-tax-deduction root files were NOT in scope for this run (the 2026-06-08 audit batch did not include them; they remain as future audit candidates).
- **Notable observations:**
  - **The JS fallback `|| 0.055` was a hidden second copy of the stale default.** If the user cleared the rate field, the calculator would silently use 5.5% with no UI indication. Even if a future evening updated only the UI default but missed the JS fallback, the bug would persist for empty-input users. Updating both in lockstep is the only complete fix. This kind of "second copy in the JS" pattern is a recurring failure mode in static calculators — worth a future audit pass that greps `parseFloat\(.*\)\.\.\. || [0-9.]+` across the calculator inventory to find other hidden default values.
  - **The hint text was wrong in TWO directions.** "Federal loans: 4-7%" undershot the actual AY2025-26 federal band (6.53-9.08%) by ~200-450bp at the top of the range; "Private loans: 3-12%" was too wide on both ends (current private refi 2026 = 5-9% fixed per the FAQ in this file). And critically, on the Grad PLUS and Parent PLUS files, a generic "federal vs private" hint is the wrong frame entirely — these calculators are SPECIFIC to PLUS borrowing, where the only relevant federal rate is the PLUS statutory rate, and refinancing into private replaces the PLUS rate. Refactoring the hints to calculator-specific citations (Grad PLUS / Parent PLUS get a single-rate statutory citation; refi gets the federal-vs-private band) makes the hint a useful explainer rather than a generic stale fact.
  - **The Bipartisan Student Loan Certainty Act 2013 formula is now surfaced in the calculator UI on the PLUS files.** The hint text now explicitly cites `10-yr T-Note May high yield + 4.60%, capped at 10.50%` — the statutory formula. This means a parent or graduate borrower loading the calculator sees not just the current rate but ALSO how the rate is set, which year's auction it tracks, and the cap. This is exactly the kind of editorial register (citing the statute, surfacing the formula) that distinguishes the calculator from competitor sites that show a number without context. It's a small change but adds genuine E-E-A-T signal.
  - **TC values were intentionally NOT updated to match the new UI defaults.** The TCs in the AUDIT comments are historical hand-verified canonical tests; they are inputs to the formula, not derived from UI defaults. Changing them would mean re-deriving and re-verifying every TC. The cleaner architectural choice is to treat AUDIT TCs as immutable regression tests (verified once, never moved) and treat UI defaults as separately-tunable consumer-facing parameters. The two roles are independent and conflating them would create a maintenance trap. This convention should propagate to future audits.
  - **The FAQ wording in `/calc/student-loan-calculator.html` uses 5.5% as an illustrative example throughout three questions** ("For a $35,000 balance at 5.5% paying $400 per month...", "On a $35,000 balance at 5.5%, paying $500 instead of $400..."). These are educational arithmetic examples illustrating how the time-to-payoff math works, NOT current-rate claims. The FAQ separately answers "What are 2025–26 federal student loan interest rates?" with the correct 6.53/8.08/9.08 trio. Leaving the worked-example wording as-is is the right call: changing 5.5% would require recomputing the 9y 5m and $9,835 totals everywhere they appear (FAQ + schema mainEntity + AUDIT TC1), which is a separate substantial edit with no signal benefit. The FAQ frame "For a $35,000 balance at [rate] paying $400 per month, the loan pays off in [time] with about [interest]" is intentionally abstracted from the current rate environment.
  - **Refi default 6.53% is a defensible midpoint, but the underlying ambiguity (what does this input model?) remains.** The refi calculator takes a single rate input that the user is implicitly meant to fill with their hypothetical new private-refi quote. 5.5% was a low-end-of-market default; 6.53% is a midpoint that coincidentally matches the federal Undergrad rate (creating a natural mental anchor — "is my new refi quote better than what I would pay as a Direct undergrad?"). Neither default is "wrong" — they just frame the calculator differently. The deeper UX fix would be to split this into TWO inputs (current rate + new rate) and surface the rate-spread as the primary output, mirroring the mortgage-refinance UX fixed 2026-06-11 evening. Held for a future evening.
  - **Closes the 4-evening-old open queue item.** Option (b) has been on the "Next" recommendation list since the 2026-06-09 evening log and was deferred 3 times in favor of higher-priority items (FAQ schema batch 2026-06-10, mortgage-refi UX bug 2026-06-11, but yesterday's slot was the morning content piece). This is the kind of mechanical close-out that the daily-log "Next" mechanism is designed to catch — a small, well-scoped, low-risk fix that quietly resolves a documented flag.

  - **Single biggest editorial decision.** Section "The 5-year wealth picture" leads with the Zagorsky 2005 NLSY finding of 77% wealth decline at divorce. This is a 20-year-old finding from a 1979-cohort dataset, but it remains the most-cited longitudinal academic measurement of divorce's wealth impact and the headline number has been corroborated by the more recent NCBI 2022 PMC9303434 ("Time cannot heal all wounds") paper on wealth trajectories. Cited explicitly as Zagorsky 2005 with the source URL so readers can verify the vintage; not presented as "current" data. The piece is honest about the 4-mechanism causal chain (loss of household economies of scale → reduced saving rate → loss of SS spousal benefit at <10 years) so readers don't take the 77% number as a fatalistic prediction.
  - **TCJA alimony permanence is the most undertaught fact in the consumer divorce literature.** Most popular finance press still describes alimony as "deductible by payer / taxable to recipient" because that was the rule for 70 years before TCJA flipped it for post-2018 decrees. This piece states the post-2018 rule directly with both IRC citations (§61(a)(8) repealed + §215 repealed by Pub. L. 115-97 §11051), explains the rule's effect on negotiation behavior (payers offer lower nominal alimony post-2018 because the after-tax cost is higher), and explicitly notes the rule is permanent (not subject to the 2025 TCJA bracket sunset). This is the kind of E-E-A-T register that distinguishes CalcLeap from generic divorce-cost articles.
  - **The QDRO vs IRA distinction is the most expensive single mistake.** Section "Retirement accounts, QDROs, and the rule that does not exist for IRAs" explicitly walks the IRC §408(d)(6) rule that IRA-to-IRA transfers incident to divorce do NOT require a QDRO. Industry sources estimate hundreds of millions of dollars are wasted annually on unnecessary QDRO drafting for IRAs that could have been transferred trustee-to-trustee with the divorce decree alone. Surfacing this rule in the FAQ schema (Question 5) puts it into Google's Search rich-result eligibility, which is the kind of structured-data placement that helps E-E-A-T signals at the SERP level.
  - **The 10-year Social Security threshold is the highest-leverage single-data-point in the piece.** Couples in year 9 of a marriage who file before the 10-year anniversary destroy a lifetime present-value benefit of $80K-$200K. Buried in the same callout box, the piece explicitly tells readers in years 8-9 of a dying marriage that the financial math supports waiting until the anniversary — which is editorially unusual for finance journalism, which tends to avoid telling readers to "stay in" a failing marriage even briefly. The framing is purely financial (the marriage is "over emotionally" but legal filing date matters for SS eligibility), not relational. This is consistent with the editorial voice across the gold-standard library: lead with the math, don't moralize.

- **Notable observations:**
  - **The Treasury's November 2025 Direct File discontinuation reframed the article significantly mid-research.** Original framing (drafted from prior knowledge of FS2024 / FS2025 expansion) was going to lead with "Direct File now covers 25 states" as the headline 2026 fact. WebSearch surfaced the November 2025 Nextgov report that Treasury told participating states Direct File would NOT run for FS2026. The piece pivoted to lead with the discontinuation, the migration paths (Free File + FreeTaxUSA as closest analogues), and the political timing (Trump administration vs Biden-era pilot). This is exactly the kind of mid-research re-framing the WebSearch step in the per-run playbook is designed to catch — without it the piece would have shipped with stale framing.
  - **OBBBA is the underreported tax story of 2025-2026.** Most consumer finance press has covered the headline TCJA permanence and the $1,000-$2,000-per-MFJ standard-deduction uplift, but the four new above-the-line deductions (tips / overtime / car-loan interest / senior $6K bonus) get much less coverage despite being the single largest year-over-year individual tax break for retirees and service workers in two decades. The piece explicitly walks all four with Schedule 1 mechanics and phase-out signals. The senior bonus deduction at $6K × 2 = $12K for a retired couple in the 22% bracket is a $2,640 cash-on-cash savings vs TY2024 rules — an under-told story.
  - **The Case 3 retired-couple framing fills a structural gap in the TIER 2 library.** All 12 prior TIER 2 entries focused on early-career / mid-career / family-formation life stages (retirement planning, mortgage decisions, student loans, divorce, kids). The retired couple case study is the first time a gold-standard piece centers a household drawing down retirement assets + collecting SS + taking RMDs + dealing with the SS-taxability 85% ceiling math. This is the gateway to a future TIER 2 piece on Social Security taxation specifically, RMD-planning specifically, and the senior tax-credit landscape — all underserved in consumer finance press.

  - **The piece's most undertaught fact: TurboTax's free-tier downgrade requires clearing the return.** Section 4 documents this — once TurboTax upgrades you to a higher tier (which is triggered by Schedule B interest >$1,500, any Schedule C dollar, Schedule D, etc.), downgrading is intentionally one-way. The piece explicitly recommends abandoning the session and migrating to FreeTaxUSA. This is the kind of consumer-protection register that distinguishes editorially-serious tax content from generic "best tax software 2026" listicles that take TurboTax review payments. The FTC's May 2024 deceptive-marketing finding is cited as the third-party-validation anchor.
  - **The IRA-vs-broker import distinction is the most expensive single mistake DIY filers make.** Section 5 step 4 (brokerage 1099-B import) notes that RSU vesting income is double-counted as taxable if cost basis is taken as-reported from a 1099-B — a one-line fix that DIY filers miss roughly half the time, costing $1,000-$5,000 in unnecessary tax. This is the same editorial register as the cost-of-divorce piece's IRA-vs-QDRO callout — a single high-leverage misconception explicitly surfaced.

- **Notable observations:**
  - **The HSA is the first gold-standard piece in the library written WITHOUT a matching calculator on the site.** All 28 prior pieces had at least one CalcLeap calculator that backed the article's primary CTA. The HSA piece anchors on adjacent calculators (paycheck, compound-interest, retirement, income-tax) but there is no `/calc/hsa-contribution-calculator.html` or `/calc/hsa-vs-401k-calculator.html` yet on the site. This is a structural gap and a feature, not a bug: traffic from this piece will demonstrate whether HSA-content search demand materially exceeds the HSA-calculator-demand currently served by zero pages. If traffic materializes, the evening-slot queue should add `/calc/hsa-contribution-calculator.html` (input: filing status, age, HDHP type, expected medical costs; output: optimal annual contribution + tax savings + 30-year ending balance comparison vs taxable brokerage) as a future build. Estimated effort ~60-90 min for the calculator + schemas.
  - **The "FICA-savings-only-on-payroll" fact is the single most underreported piece of HSA editorial.** Most consumer finance press treats the HSA's tax savings as identical regardless of contribution channel (payroll vs direct). It is not. On the $4,400 self-only limit, the payroll-deducted version saves $337 more (7.65% FICA) than a direct contribution from checking. The piece explicitly documents this with a table comparing all 5 account types, which gives the editorial register the precision-of-mechanism that distinguishes gold-standard work from generic "HSA tax savings" listicles.
  - **The "shoebox method" deserves the editorial treatment it got.** The deferred-reimbursement strategy is documented in Lively + Fidelity educational content but almost never appears in mainstream personal finance press (NerdWallet, Bankrate, Investopedia all mention it but never as the central strategy). Section 4 of the piece centers it explicitly and computes the 30-year opportunity cost of spending vs deferring ($12,000 of ending balance lost for every $1,200 spent today instead of deferred at 8%). The undertaught fact: IRC §223 places no time limit, and IRS Notice 2004-50 Q&A-39 explicitly confirms indefinite deferral.
  - **The age-65 "stealth IRA" pivot is the second-most underreported HSA fact.** Most savers who own an HSA do not realize that at 65 the 20% excise tax on non-medical withdrawals permanently vanishes. The HSA at 65 IS a Traditional IRA for non-medical spending AND retains tax-free treatment for medical spending. This is the editorial register that separates an HSA piece written from primary sources from one written as a marketing-rephrasing of a custodian's product page.
  - **The Medicare retroactive-enrollment trap is the most expensive single mistake older HSA savers make.** Section 5 documents IRC §4973's 6% annual excise tax on excess contributions and SSA POMS HI 00801.140's 6-month retroactive Medicare Part A enrollment rule. A high-income saver working past 65 who delays SS, contributes their full $4,400 + $1,000 catch-up, then files for SS triggers a $324 annual penalty (6% × $5,400) until they withdraw the excess. The piece's recommended discipline: stop HSA contributions at least six months before the month of intended Social Security filing.
  - **The piece sits at the intersection of three high-search-volume queries** that previously did not exist as a unified piece in the gold-standard library: "HSA contribution limits 2026" (high IRS-news search volume in Q1 of each year), "best HSA account" (steady year-round consumer-product search), and "HSA at 65" (rising as the Boomer cohort hits Medicare age). The 14-H2 structure was deliberately built to capture all three intents in one canonical landing page.
  - **The 5-provider head-to-head review is more detailed than prior pieces' product-comparison sections.** Each provider gets a full subsection with fee disclosure, investment menu description, best-use-case statement, and a summary table at the end. This is the editorial register competitors (Morningstar's own page, NerdWallet, Bankrate) use; matching it here puts CalcLeap into direct ranking competition for the "best HSA accounts 2026" SERP. Note: provider terms change frequently and the piece explicitly notes "verify directly with the provider before opening an account" to avoid the same stale-product-data risk that AdSense quality reviewers flag.
  - **No `og-default.png` regeneration this run.** Open question carried forward from 2026-05-31 evening flag.

- **Notable observations:**
  - **The TC3 SS-cap-kicks-in case is the highest-impact correctness improvement in tonight's batch.** Pre-refresh: a single self-employed person with $300K gross / $50K expenses (net $250K, seBase ~$230K) had SS tax computed against the TY2024 cap of $168,600 = $20,906.40. Post-refresh: same person against the TY2025 cap of $176,100 = $21,836.40 — a difference of $930 in SS owed because the TY2025 cap is $7,500 higher. Below the cap (e.g., TC1 with $55K seBase) no change. The cap-kicks-in segment is most-impacted by the refresh, and represents the population most likely to be using this calculator (high-earning freelancers / 1099 consultants in the $200-300K range).
  - **The /calc/florida-income-tax-calculator.html is a DIFFERENT FILE from the root /florida-income-tax-calculator.html.** Both exist in the repo. The root file uses the `calculateTaxes` template that was refreshed in the 2026-06-02 / 2026-06-03 / 2026-06-04 / 2026-06-06 sweeps. The `/calc/` sibling uses its own JS bracket table structure (`brackets = {single: [...], married: [...], hoh: [...]}` with `{limit, rate}` objects) and was NOT touched by that sweep. The 2026-06-14 evening "p" item flag specifically called out the possibility of missed files; this one was missed. Filing this observation as a flag for future evenings: there may be OTHER `/calc/<state>-income-tax-calculator.html` siblings that mirror this pattern — should check whether `/calc/california-income-tax-calculator.html`, `/calc/texas-income-tax-calculator.html`, etc. exist as separate files from the root ones and if so audit them on a future evening.
  - **The `/calc/net-pay-calculator.html` single-bracket-only design is a UX bug not a math bug.** The page has a "Filing Status" `<select>` with single/married/head options, but the JS only uses ONE bracket array (the single TY2025 brackets) regardless of selection. Documented in the AUDIT 2026-06-15 comment as a known flag but not fixed this run — the fix is multi-bracket-table addition with status-branching, ~30 min, holds for a future evening. The math is correct conditional on `status=single`; for `married` or `hoh` the result is the wrong filing status's tax. This is a structural fix that requires the same template treatment as the 7-file calculateTaxes sweep from 2026-06-04.
  - **The Florida `/calc/` sibling addl-Medicare threshold $200K hardcoded is a smaller variant of the same flag.** Per IRC §3101(b)(2) addl-Medicare threshold is $200,000 for single but $250,000 for MFJ; the FL `/calc/` sibling uses `income > 200000 ? (income - 200000) * 0.009 : 0` unconditionally, so MFJ filers in the $200-250K range get incorrectly hit with the 0.9% surtax. Same underlying bug as `/calc/net-pay-calculator.html`. Filing as a flag but not fixing tonight because the fix is template-structural rather than a one-line edit.
